Manufacturing English

A manufacturing English curriculum for production meetings, line problems, lean improvement, defects, maintenance, safety, supplier quality, shift handoffs, and root-cause communication.

Words into action

For plant managers, production supervisors, quality engineers, maintenance teams, industrial engineers, safety leads, supply planners, and manufacturing-adjacent professionals.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Report an output shortfall precisely while separating observed downtime, unconfirmed causes, and different performance measures.
  • Describe observed waiting, challenge unsupported blame, and define a bounded improvement trial without claiming savings in advance.
  • Reconcile output categories and explain yield, pending recovery, and scrap without double-counting or assuming a rework outcome.
  • Challenge a premature root-cause statement, preserve competing hypotheses, and distinguish containment from verified corrective action.
  • Explain schedule conflicts, distinguish work types, and present conditional options without assuming authority to defer maintenance or restart equipment.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

Original fictional language practice, not equipment-operation, maintenance, engineering, or safety instructions. Figures, organizations, and workplace cases are invented. Actual work must follow applicable law, approved site procedures, trained personnel, and authorized decisions. Classroom safety discussions do not authorize isolation, servicing, or restart.

01Production Flow and Daily ManagementEight hundred forty is not the whole story+

Your goal

Report an output shortfall precisely while separating observed downtime, unconfirmed causes, and different performance measures.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Eight hundred forty is not the whole story

Production lead Mei and operations manager Daniel review yesterday's line output: 840 completed units against a 1,000-unit target. The log records 40 minutes of downtime, but the cause and its contribution to the shortfall are still being checked. The briefing supplies no planned production time, ideal cycle time, or quality breakdown. A dashboard labels 84% as overall equipment effectiveness. Mei and Daniel must correct the metric and give a useful update without claiming the downtime explains every missing unit.

Mei: Production lead / Daniel: Operations manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was the output shortfall?

Answer and explanation

D. 160 units

Subtracting 840 completed units from the 1,000-unit target gives a 160-unit shortfall.

2. What does 84% represent here?

Answer and explanation

A. Output as a share of the stated target

The available calculation is 840 divided by 1,000, which measures output target attainment.

3. What is known about the downtime?

Answer and explanation

C. Forty minutes were logged; its cause and contribution remain under review.

The briefing gives a duration but not the cause or a complete loss attribution.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

840 divided by 1,000 is 84% target attainment. The shortfall is 160 units, or 16% of target. Neither result supplies OEE, availability, or first-pass yield. Those measures require their own defined inputs and calculation bases.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
production target
The planned output quantity for a defined period.set a production target
actual output
The quantity actually produced in the stated period.report actual output
target attainment
Actual output expressed relative to the stated target.calculate target attainment
shortfall
The amount by which a result falls below its target.quantify the shortfall
throughput
The quantity passing through a process per unit of time.measure throughput
cycle time
The elapsed time required for a defined process cycle.measure cycle time
takt time
Available production time divided by required demand for the same period.calculate takt time
lead time
The elapsed time from a defined request or start to completion.reduce lead time
work in process
Material that has entered production but is not yet finished.control work in process
bottleneck
The step that limits the output of the overall process.identify the bottleneck
constraint
A limiting factor on the system's performance.manage the constraint
line balance
Distribution of work across production steps.improve line balance
downtime
Time during which equipment or a process is not producing as defined.log downtime
planned stop
A scheduled interruption included in the operating plan.distinguish planned stops
unplanned stop
An interruption not included in the intended operating schedule.investigate unplanned stops
availability
The share of planned production time in which equipment is running.assess equipment availability
performance loss
Output-speed loss relative to the defined ideal running rate.analyze performance losses
quality loss
Output that does not meet the defined good-product criteria.quantify quality losses
overall equipment effectiveness
Availability multiplied by performance and quality, with defined factors; abbreviated OEE.calculate overall equipment effectiveness
daily management
Routine review of performance, problems, and follow-up actions.support daily management
tier meeting
A short escalation meeting at a defined organizational level.escalate through a tier meeting
visual board
A shared display of current measures, status, and actions.update the visual board
loss attribution
Assignment of a measured loss to supported contributing factors.verify loss attribution
recovery plan
A defined proposal for addressing a performance or schedule gap.review the recovery plan

A recorded 40-minute stop does not automatically explain the entire output gap. The expected running rate, other losses, and the time basis matter. Describe the log as evidence under review rather than a completed causal analysis.

C Notice the language

A precise update can still be brief

Start with the result, then the confirmed loss information and the open cause. This lets a busy manager distinguish the size of the gap from the explanation. Avoid filling a short status slot with a confident causal claim simply because the investigation is unfinished.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the result
We completed 840 units against a target of 1,000.
Quantify the gap
The shortfall is 160 units, or 16% of target.
Name the measure
Eighty-four percent is output target attainment.
Correct the label
The supplied figures do not support an OEE calculation.
State the logged loss
The log records 40 minutes of downtime.
Keep cause open
The reason for that downtime is still being checked.
Limit attribution
We have not established how much of the shortfall the stop explains.
Ask for the time basis
What planned production time was used in this calculation?
Distinguish rate from quantity
Throughput needs a quantity and a time period.
Check the constraint
We have not yet confirmed which step limited total output.
Request the breakdown
Separate speed, availability, and quality information.
Avoid blame
The current log does not establish an operator cause.
Make the update useful
Report the known gap and the next evidence check separately.
Bound the recovery claim
A recovery proposal is not a guarantee that the missing output will be recovered.
Assign the follow-up
Mei will reconcile the log and report the supported loss breakdown.
Close the board update
Keep the metric definition beside the percentage.
6 language and precision notes
Against
Introduces the comparison target, not necessarily customer demand.
Down by
State the amount and comparison period to avoid ambiguity.
Percent of
Identifies a ratio; it is not the same as a percentage-point change.
Explains
Makes a causal or accounting claim that needs support.
Logged
Means recorded, not necessarily investigated or verified.
Recover
Specify output, schedule, or service; one recovery may not imply another.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which board entry is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. 840 units completed; 84% of target; 160-unit shortfall.

The entry uses only the supplied output and target and keeps units distinct from time.

2. Why is OEE not established?

Answer and explanation

B. Planned time, ideal cycle time, and quality information are not supplied.

OEE combines distinct factors whose required inputs are missing from the brief.

3. A manager converts the 40-minute stop into the full 160-unit shortfall. What is missing from that attribution?

Answer and explanation

A. A supported running-rate and loss breakdown linking the stop to the output gap

The count gap is known, but minutes cannot become lost units without a relevant rate and loss basis. Other speed, quality, or operating losses may also contribute.

4. If a second shift produces 900 against a target of 1,000, what is its attainment?

Answer and explanation

A. 90%, six percentage points above 84%

900 divided by 1,000 is 90%; the difference between 90% and 84% is six percentage points.

E Conversations

Eight hundred forty is not the whole story

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: Production lead / Daniel: Operations manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • downtime
  • performance loss
  • target attainment
  • shortfall
  • visual board
  • recovery plan
  • Quality loss
  • bottleneck
  • availability
  • Loss attribution
  1. Daniel
    Yesterday's board shows eight hundred forty units against a thousand. It labels eighty-four percent as OEE, but I cannot see the inputs behind that label.
  2. Mei
    The figure is 1. We divided completed output by the stated target; we did not calculate the availability, performance, and quality factors.
  3. Daniel
    Please change the heading before the morning meeting. What is the gap in units, and what can we actually say about the stop?
  4. Mei
    The 2 is one hundred sixty units, or sixteen percent of target. That is a quantity gap, not a duration and not an explanation of the cause.
  5. Daniel
    The stop log records forty minutes. Does the team believe that interruption accounts for all the missing units, or is the analysis still open?
  6. Mei
    The 3 is logged, but its cause and contribution remain under review. We should not convert forty minutes into one hundred sixty lost units without the relevant basis.
  7. Daniel
    We would need to know the expected production rate and whether other losses occurred. The board currently makes a single interruption look like a complete explanation.
  8. Mei
    Exactly. 4 needs supporting data. I will reconcile the timing, output, and other relevant records rather than attach the entire gap to the most visible event.
  9. Daniel
    Which inputs do you need for the equipment measure? Give me the missing fields so I can ask the line team for them.
  10. Mei
    Start with the basis for 5. We need the planned production time and the relevant running time, not merely the output target and one stop duration.
  11. Daniel
    The speed side also needs a clear reference. Actual output can fall short even while the equipment is running, so time and quantity should not be merged.
  12. Mei
    Yes. The 6 assessment needs the defined ideal rate and actual running performance. The supplied briefing does not provide that basis, so the board cannot claim a completed result.
  13. Daniel
    And the completed-unit count does not tell us how many met the quality definition. We should not silently assume that every produced unit was good.
  14. Mei
    Correct. 7 needs the appropriate output breakdown. A production count by itself cannot establish that no defects or other quality losses occurred.
  15. Daniel
    Someone has suggested the packing station is the limiting step. That may be worth checking, but I have not seen evidence showing it constrained the whole line.
  16. Mei
    Keep 8 as an investigation question. A busy station or visible queue does not automatically identify the system's actual limiting step.
  17. Daniel
    For today's meeting, I need a concise update: result, known interruption, open questions, and responsibility for the next check. We can discuss recovery after that evidence is clearer.
  18. Mei
    I will update the 9 accordingly. It will show the output gap and logged stop separately, with me responsible for reconciling the records and reporting the supported breakdown.
  19. Daniel
    Good. We can acknowledge the missed target without guessing why it happened. Any proposal to recover output should make its assumptions and constraints clear.
  20. Mei
    Agreed. The 10 will remain a proposal until assessed and authorized. First, the board must use the right measure and keep the unfinished investigation visible.

Answers and explanations
  1. target attainment Turn 2. The calculation compares actual output with the target, not the separate inputs needed for OEE.
  2. shortfall Turn 4. The shortfall is the target minus actual output: 1,000 minus 840 equals 160 units.
  3. downtime Turn 6. Downtime is the recorded nonproducing duration, whose cause and contribution remain unconfirmed here.
  4. Loss attribution Turn 8. Loss attribution assigns losses to supported contributors; the log alone does not complete that analysis.
  5. availability Turn 10. Availability relates running time to planned production time, which is not supplied in this brief.
  6. performance loss Turn 12. Performance loss concerns running below the defined ideal rate, requiring speed-related information beyond downtime.
  7. Quality loss Turn 14. Quality loss concerns units not meeting the defined good-product criteria, information absent from the supplied count.
  8. bottleneck Turn 16. A bottleneck is the system-limiting step, which must be identified from evidence rather than a local impression.
  9. visual board Turn 18. The visual board should distinguish the confirmed measures, unresolved causes, and assigned next actions.
  10. recovery plan Turn 20. A recovery plan is a proposal for addressing the gap, not proof that the missing output will be recovered.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Daniel: Yesterday's board shows eight hundred forty units against a thousand. It labels eighty-four percent as OEE, but I cannot see the inputs behind that label.
  2. Mei: The figure is target attainment. We divided completed output by the stated target; we did not calculate the availability, performance, and quality factors.
  3. Daniel: Please change the heading before the morning meeting. What is the gap in units, and what can we actually say about the stop?
  4. Mei: The shortfall is one hundred sixty units, or sixteen percent of target. That is a quantity gap, not a duration and not an explanation of the cause.
  5. Daniel: The stop log records forty minutes. Does the team believe that interruption accounts for all the missing units, or is the analysis still open?
  6. Mei: The downtime is logged, but its cause and contribution remain under review. We should not convert forty minutes into one hundred sixty lost units without the relevant basis.
  7. Daniel: We would need to know the expected production rate and whether other losses occurred. The board currently makes a single interruption look like a complete explanation.
  8. Mei: Exactly. Loss attribution needs supporting data. I will reconcile the timing, output, and other relevant records rather than attach the entire gap to the most visible event.
  9. Daniel: Which inputs do you need for the equipment measure? Give me the missing fields so I can ask the line team for them.
  10. Mei: Start with the basis for availability. We need the planned production time and the relevant running time, not merely the output target and one stop duration.
  11. Daniel: The speed side also needs a clear reference. Actual output can fall short even while the equipment is running, so time and quantity should not be merged.
  12. Mei: Yes. The performance loss assessment needs the defined ideal rate and actual running performance. The supplied briefing does not provide that basis, so the board cannot claim a completed result.
  13. Daniel: And the completed-unit count does not tell us how many met the quality definition. We should not silently assume that every produced unit was good.
  14. Mei: Correct. Quality loss needs the appropriate output breakdown. A production count by itself cannot establish that no defects or other quality losses occurred.
  15. Daniel: Someone has suggested the packing station is the limiting step. That may be worth checking, but I have not seen evidence showing it constrained the whole line.
  16. Mei: Keep bottleneck as an investigation question. A busy station or visible queue does not automatically identify the system's actual limiting step.
  17. Daniel: For today's meeting, I need a concise update: result, known interruption, open questions, and responsibility for the next check. We can discuss recovery after that evidence is clearer.
  18. Mei: I will update the visual board accordingly. It will show the output gap and logged stop separately, with me responsible for reconciling the records and reporting the supported breakdown.
  19. Daniel: Good. We can acknowledge the missed target without guessing why it happened. Any proposal to recover output should make its assumptions and constraints clear.
  20. Mei: Agreed. The recovery plan will remain a proposal until assessed and authorized. First, the board must use the right measure and keep the unfinished investigation visible.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A 160-unit gap with downtime still under review

Fictional daily production meeting: the line made 840 units against a 1,000-unit target; 40 minutes of downtime are logged, but the cause is unconfirmed.

  1. Production supervisor: We produced eight hundred forty units against a thousand-unit target, leaving a gap of one hundred sixty.
  2. Operations manager: Does the forty minutes of downtime explain the entire gap to target?
  3. Production supervisor: That hasn't been established. Downtime is recorded, but its cause and contribution are still under review.
  4. Operations manager: What can we report as completed work rather than an assumption about the constraint?
  5. Production supervisor: The output count and downtime duration are confirmed. The explanation for the shortfall remains pending.
  6. Operations manager: Should we promise to recover all one hundred sixty units during the next shift?
  7. Production supervisor: Not without assessing capacity and the unresolved issue. I can request a recovery-plan review.
  8. Operations manager: Please include cycle-time evidence if it helps explain the actual throughput limitation.
  9. Production supervisor: I'll bring the verified log and separate measured performance from proposed explanations.
  10. Operations manager: Good. We'll review the next update before committing to a recovery quantity or naming the cause.
A queue grows between assembly and inspection

Separate fictional production case: assembly completed 120 units, inspection completed 90, and 30 newly assembled units await inspection; no earlier queue is included.

  1. Line leader: Assembly completed one hundred twenty units, and inspection completed ninety from this group.
  2. Production planner: Then can I describe all one hundred twenty as finished output for dispatch?
  3. Line leader: No. Thirty still await inspection, so assembly completion is not the same as dispatch readiness.
  4. Production planner: Does that prove inspection is our permanent production constraint rather than a temporary queue?
  5. Line leader: Not from this snapshot. We need the process history and capacity information before making that conclusion.
  6. Production planner: I'll report the thirty-unit inspection queue separately from the completed inspection count.
  7. Line leader: Please keep the scope clear: these figures concern this group, not an earlier backlog.
  8. Production planner: Can you confirm the next inspection-status checkpoint before I update the downstream schedule?
  9. Line leader: I'll ask the inspection lead and return a verified update rather than estimate an unapproved release time.
  10. Production planner: Understood. I'll keep throughput stages distinct and avoid treating unfinished work as available inventory.
The line restarted, but the loss is not explained

Separate fictional daily-management exercise: line B restarted at 11:10 after a 15-minute stop; the stop reason and lost-output estimate remain unverified.

  1. Shift supervisor: Line B restarted at eleven ten after a fifteen-minute stop. The cause is still being checked.
  2. Production analyst: Can I close the downtime investigation now that the line is running again?
  3. Shift supervisor: Restart is a confirmed operational status, not a completed explanation of the stop.
  4. Production analyst: Should I calculate lost output using yesterday's cycle time as though it applied today?
  5. Shift supervisor: Only as an explicitly labeled estimate if appropriate; today's actual conditions haven't been verified here.
  6. Production analyst: I'll keep the confirmed duration separate from any proposed loss calculation.
  7. Shift supervisor: Good. Don't describe the restart as proof that the underlying constraint has disappeared permanently.
  8. Production analyst: What next update do you need for the afternoon daily-management discussion?
  9. Shift supervisor: The verified reason, relevant production conditions, and any remaining action owned by the responsible team.
  10. Production analyst: I'll gather those details and report completed recovery separately from the pending investigation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Eight hundred forty is not the whole story
  1. Read the production review. State 840 against 1,000, 84% target attainment, and a 160-unit shortfall.
  2. Swap roles. Keep the forty-minute stop separate from its unconfirmed cause and output effect; do not label 84% as OEE.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Compare the right percentages

A line produces 450 units against a target of 500. It logs 20 minutes of downtime. No cause or OEE inputs are supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lead: "Target attainment is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

D. ninety

Dividing 450 by 500 gives 0.90, or ninety percent.

2. Planner: "The output shortfall is ___ units."

Answer and explanation

B. fifty

The target of 500 minus actual output of 450 leaves fifty units.

3. Lead: "Twenty minutes describes recorded ___."

Answer and explanation

A. downtime

The supplied duration is nonproducing time, not a quantity of missing units.

4. Planner: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unconfirmed

The briefing provides no established explanation for the recorded stop.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Manufacturing English
Professional audience: plant managers, production supervisors, quality engineers, maintenance teams, industrial engineers, safety leads, supply planners, and manufacturing-adjacent professionals
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not equipment-operation, maintenance, engineering, or safety instructions. Figures, organizations, and workplace cases are invented. Actual work must follow applicable law, approved site procedures, trained personnel, and authorized decisions. Classroom safety discussions do not authorize isolation, servicing, or restart.
Lesson 01: Production Flow and Daily Management
Original fictional case: Production lead Mei and operations manager Daniel review yesterday's line output: 840 completed units against a 1,000-unit target. The log records 40 minutes of downtime, but the cause and its contribution to the shortfall are still being checked. The briefing supplies no planned production time, ideal cycle time, or quality breakdown. A dashboard labels 84% as overall equipment effectiveness. Mei and Daniel must correct the metric and give a useful update without claiming the downtime explains every missing unit.
Language workshop: Eight hundred forty is not the whole story
Communication goal: Report an output shortfall precisely while separating observed downtime, unconfirmed causes, and different performance measures.
Language guidance: 840 divided by 1,000 is 84% target attainment. The shortfall is 160 units, or 16% of target. Neither result supplies OEE, availability, or first-pass yield. Those measures require their own defined inputs and calculation bases.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We completed 840 units against a target of 1,000. / The shortfall is 160 units, or 16% of target. / Eighty-four percent is output target attainment. / The supplied figures do not support an OEE calculation. / The log records 40 minutes of downtime. / The reason for that downtime is still being checked. / We have not established how much of the shortfall the stop explains. / What planned production time was used in this calculation? / Throughput needs a quantity and a time period. / We have not yet confirmed which step limited total output. / Separate speed, availability, and quality information. / The current log does not establish an operator cause. / Report the known gap and the next evidence check separately. / A recovery proposal is not a guarantee that the missing output will be recovered. / Mei will reconcile the log and report the supported loss breakdown. / Keep the metric definition beside the percentage.
Vocabulary: production target: The planned output quantity for a defined period. / actual output: The quantity actually produced in the stated period. / target attainment: Actual output expressed relative to the stated target. / shortfall: The amount by which a result falls below its target. / throughput: The quantity passing through a process per unit of time. / cycle time: The elapsed time required for a defined process cycle. / takt time: Available production time divided by required demand for the same period. / lead time: The elapsed time from a defined request or start to completion. / work in process: Material that has entered production but is not yet finished. / bottleneck: The step that limits the output of the overall process. / constraint: A limiting factor on the system's performance. / line balance: Distribution of work across production steps. / downtime: Time during which equipment or a process is not producing as defined. / planned stop: A scheduled interruption included in the operating plan. / unplanned stop: An interruption not included in the intended operating schedule. / availability: The share of planned production time in which equipment is running. / performance loss: Output-speed loss relative to the defined ideal running rate. / quality loss: Output that does not meet the defined good-product criteria. / overall equipment effectiveness: Availability multiplied by performance and quality, with defined factors; abbreviated OEE. / daily management: Routine review of performance, problems, and follow-up actions. / tier meeting: A short escalation meeting at a defined organizational level. / visual board: A shared display of current measures, status, and actions. / loss attribution: Assignment of a measured loss to supported contributing factors. / recovery plan: A defined proposal for addressing a performance or schedule gap.
Speaking task: Read the production review. State 840 against 1,000, 84% target attainment, and a 160-unit shortfall. Swap roles. Keep the forty-minute stop separate from its unconfirmed cause and output effect; do not label 84% as OEE. Read the corrected 450-unit transfer. State ninety-percent attainment and a fifty-unit gap without inventing a stop cause.
Partner: Mei: Production lead / Daniel: Operations manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A line produces 450 units against a target of 500. It logs 20 minutes of downtime. No cause or OEE inputs are supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Daniel: For today's meeting, I need a concise update: result, known interruption, open questions, and responsibility for the next check. We can discuss recovery after that evidence is clearer.
Mei: I will update the visual board accordingly. It will show the output gap and logged stop separately, with me responsible for reconciling the records and reporting the supported breakdown.
Daniel: Good. We can acknowledge the missed target without guessing why it happened. Any proposal to recover output should make its assumptions and constraints clear.
Mei: Agreed. The recovery plan will remain a proposal until assessed and authorized. First, the board must use the right measure and keep the unfinished investigation visible.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Manufacturing English
Professional audience: plant managers, production supervisors, quality engineers, maintenance teams, industrial engineers, safety leads, supply planners, and manufacturing-adjacent professionals
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not equipment-operation, maintenance, engineering, or safety instructions. Figures, organizations, and workplace cases are invented. Actual work must follow applicable law, approved site procedures, trained personnel, and authorized decisions. Classroom safety discussions do not authorize isolation, servicing, or restart.
Lesson 01: Production Flow and Daily Management
Original fictional case: Production lead Mei and operations manager Daniel review yesterday's line output: 840 completed units against a 1,000-unit target. The log records 40 minutes of downtime, but the cause and its contribution to the shortfall are still being checked. The briefing supplies no planned production time, ideal cycle time, or quality breakdown. A dashboard labels 84% as overall equipment effectiveness. Mei and Daniel must correct the metric and give a useful update without claiming the downtime explains every missing unit.
Language workshop: Eight hundred forty is not the whole story
Communication goal: Report an output shortfall precisely while separating observed downtime, unconfirmed causes, and different performance measures.
Language guidance: 840 divided by 1,000 is 84% target attainment. The shortfall is 160 units, or 16% of target. Neither result supplies OEE, availability, or first-pass yield. Those measures require their own defined inputs and calculation bases.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We completed 840 units against a target of 1,000. / The shortfall is 160 units, or 16% of target. / Eighty-four percent is output target attainment. / The supplied figures do not support an OEE calculation. / The log records 40 minutes of downtime. / The reason for that downtime is still being checked. / We have not established how much of the shortfall the stop explains. / What planned production time was used in this calculation? / Throughput needs a quantity and a time period. / We have not yet confirmed which step limited total output. / Separate speed, availability, and quality information. / The current log does not establish an operator cause. / Report the known gap and the next evidence check separately. / A recovery proposal is not a guarantee that the missing output will be recovered. / Mei will reconcile the log and report the supported loss breakdown. / Keep the metric definition beside the percentage.
Vocabulary: production target: The planned output quantity for a defined period. / actual output: The quantity actually produced in the stated period. / target attainment: Actual output expressed relative to the stated target. / shortfall: The amount by which a result falls below its target. / throughput: The quantity passing through a process per unit of time. / cycle time: The elapsed time required for a defined process cycle. / takt time: Available production time divided by required demand for the same period. / lead time: The elapsed time from a defined request or start to completion. / work in process: Material that has entered production but is not yet finished. / bottleneck: The step that limits the output of the overall process. / constraint: A limiting factor on the system's performance. / line balance: Distribution of work across production steps. / downtime: Time during which equipment or a process is not producing as defined. / planned stop: A scheduled interruption included in the operating plan. / unplanned stop: An interruption not included in the intended operating schedule. / availability: The share of planned production time in which equipment is running. / performance loss: Output-speed loss relative to the defined ideal running rate. / quality loss: Output that does not meet the defined good-product criteria. / overall equipment effectiveness: Availability multiplied by performance and quality, with defined factors; abbreviated OEE. / daily management: Routine review of performance, problems, and follow-up actions. / tier meeting: A short escalation meeting at a defined organizational level. / visual board: A shared display of current measures, status, and actions. / loss attribution: Assignment of a measured loss to supported contributing factors. / recovery plan: A defined proposal for addressing a performance or schedule gap.
Speaking task: Read the production review. State 840 against 1,000, 84% target attainment, and a 160-unit shortfall. Swap roles. Keep the forty-minute stop separate from its unconfirmed cause and output effect; do not label 84% as OEE. Read the corrected 450-unit transfer. State ninety-percent attainment and a fifty-unit gap without inventing a stop cause.
Partner: Mei: Production lead / Daniel: Operations manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A line produces 450 units against a target of 500. It logs 20 minutes of downtime. No cause or OEE inputs are supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Daniel: For today's meeting, I need a concise update: result, known interruption, open questions, and responsibility for the next check. We can discuss recovery after that evidence is clearer.
Mei: I will update the visual board accordingly. It will show the output gap and logged stop separately, with me responsible for reconciling the records and reporting the supported breakdown.
Daniel: Good. We can acknowledge the missed target without guessing why it happened. Any proposal to recover output should make its assumptions and constraints clear.
Mei: Agreed. The recovery plan will remain a proposal until assessed and authorized. First, the board must use the right measure and keep the unfinished investigation visible.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

02Lean, Waste, and Continuous ImprovementThe queue is for a shared tool+

Your goal

Describe observed waiting, challenge unsupported blame, and define a bounded improvement trial without claiming savings in advance.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The queue is for a shared tool

Improvement facilitator Ana and supervisor Marcus observe a one-hour assembly session. Eight waiting episodes for a shared tool total 24 operator-minutes; some episodes overlap, so this is not 24 minutes of line downtime. The supervisor calls the operators slow. The team proposes a one-bench point-of-use tool trial, but no trial result exists. Ana and Marcus must distinguish waiting from working pace, explain the time basis, and keep safety and quality checks unchanged in the proposal.

Ana: Improvement facilitator / Marcus: Assembly supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was observed?

Answer and explanation

D. Eight tool-waiting episodes totaling 24 operator-minutes

The observation concerns waiting episodes and accumulated operator time, not a completed improvement or individual performance diagnosis.

2. Why is 24 not necessarily line downtime?

Answer and explanation

B. Some waiting episodes overlap, and the measure is operator-minutes.

Overlapping individual waits cannot simply be added and labeled elapsed whole-line downtime.

3. What is the proposed change?

Answer and explanation

B. A one-bench point-of-use tool trial with safety and quality checks unchanged

The scenario supplies a bounded trial proposal, not a result or permission to remove controls.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twenty-four operator-minutes across eight episodes averages three operator-minutes per episode. Overlapping waits mean that total is not automatically elapsed line downtime. State the observation period and time basis when reporting the result.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
lean manufacturing
An approach to improving customer value by reducing waste in the process.apply lean manufacturing
waste
Resource use that does not add the defined value or support a necessary requirement.identify process waste
waiting
Time when work cannot proceed because a needed input is unavailable.reduce waiting time
motion
Movement of people within a task or process.analyze unnecessary motion
transportation
Movement of material between locations or steps.reduce unnecessary transportation
overproduction
Producing earlier or in greater quantity than needed.prevent overproduction
overprocessing
Performing more work than the relevant requirement needs.identify overprocessing
excess inventory
Material held beyond the justified operating need.reduce excess inventory
kaizen
Focused, ongoing improvement of work processes.run a kaizen event
gemba
The actual place where the work occurs.observe at the gemba
value stream
The connected activities and information involved in delivering a product.map the value stream
current-state map
A representation of how the process currently works.create a current-state map
future-state map
A proposed representation of an improved process.develop a future-state map
point-of-use storage
Keeping needed items near where they are used.trial point-of-use storage
5S
Workplace organization through sort, set in order, shine, standardize, and sustain.sustain 5S practices
standard work
The agreed method and conditions for performing a process.update standard work
pull system
Replenishment or production triggered by downstream need.design a pull system
kanban
A signal authorizing defined replenishment or production in a pull system.use a kanban signal
work-cell layout
The arrangement of people, equipment, and material in a production cell.assess the work-cell layout
operator-minute
One minute of one operator's time, accumulated across people if specified.report operator-minutes
baseline observation
A measurement of the current process before a proposed change.record a baseline observation
pilot trial
A limited test of a proposed change before broader adoption.define a pilot trial
countermeasure
An action intended to address an identified problem.test a countermeasure
sustainment
Maintaining an improvement over time through defined follow-up.plan sustainment

Point-of-use storage is a proposed countermeasure, not proven savings. A bounded trial should define what is compared while retaining required safety and quality controls. A successful local trial would still need review before broader adoption.

C Notice the language

Invite the people who do the work

A respectful observation describes the delay and asks how the process creates it. Operators can explain practical constraints that a meeting-room proposal misses. Their involvement is not permission to bypass controls; it helps the team design a realistic, reviewable improvement trial.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Describe the delay
Operators waited for the shared tool during eight observed episodes.
Name the time basis
The total is 24 operator-minutes, not 24 minutes of line downtime.
Separate pace from access
Waiting for a tool does not by itself show slow working pace.
Challenge the attribution
The observation does not establish that operator speed caused the delay.
Invite practical detail
Ask the operators where the tool is needed and what constrains access.
Define the current state
Record the present locations, demand, and waiting pattern.
State the proposal
Trial point-of-use storage at one bench.
Bound the claim
No improvement result is available yet.
Preserve controls
Required safety and quality checks remain unchanged.
Compare consistently
Use the same time basis and comparable task conditions.
Check for displaced problems
Confirm that the change does not create a shortage at another bench.
Separate a trial from rollout
A one-bench test is not a factory-wide implementation.
Avoid prebooked savings
Do not report proposed time savings as already achieved.
Make the review specific
Review the waiting observations and any new difficulties after the trial.
Retain operator input
Include the people who use the tool in the practical review.
Plan the follow-through
Agree how an accepted improvement would be maintained.
6 language and precision notes
Waste
A process concept, not a label for an employee.
Slow
Can confuse working pace with delay caused by missing inputs.
Operator-minutes
Adds individual time; it may exceed elapsed clock time when waits overlap.
Trial
Tests a proposal without claiming the result beforehand.
Unchanged
Specify the controls that remain in force during the trial.
Sustained
Means an improvement persists, not merely that one test looked favorable.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which observation is defensible?

Answer and explanation

C. Eight tool-waiting episodes totaled 24 operator-minutes during the hour.

The statement preserves the actual event count, observation period, and accumulated time measure.

2. What is the average time per recorded episode?

Answer and explanation

C. Three operator-minutes

Dividing 24 operator-minutes by eight episodes gives three operator-minutes per episode.

3. Which proposal has suitable boundaries?

Answer and explanation

D. Trial tool placement at one bench, keep required controls, and compare waiting under comparable conditions.

The proposal defines a limited comparison without changing required controls or assuming the outcome.

4. Waiting falls at the trial bench while the shared tool is less available elsewhere. Which conclusion still needs evidence?

Answer and explanation

B. Total benefit across the connected process, including any displaced waiting

Moving delay can improve a local measure without improving the whole process. Compare relevant areas on a consistent basis before claiming overall benefit.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

The queue is for a shared tool

Ana: Improvement facilitator / Marcus: Assembly supervisor

  10. Ana

Answers and explanations
  1. waiting Turn 2. Waiting describes work delayed by unavailable input, not evidence of a slow working pace.
  2. operator-minute Turn 4. An operator-minute measures one person's time; overlapping waits cannot be relabeled as elapsed whole-line downtime.
  3. baseline observation Turn 6. The baseline records the current process before a change and needs a clear time and task basis.
  4. current-state map Turn 8. A current-state map represents the existing locations, activities, and information flow before proposing a changed arrangement.
  5. motion Turn 10. Motion concerns people's movements within work, distinct from moving material between locations.
  6. point-of-use storage Turn 12. Point-of-use storage places needed items near their use, here as a proposal rather than a verified result.
  7. pilot trial Turn 14. A pilot trial is a bounded test whose conditions and retained controls must be clear before interpreting results.
  8. countermeasure Turn 16. A countermeasure is an action intended to address a problem; it does not establish achieved savings before testing.
  9. value stream Turn 18. The value stream includes connected activities, so a local change must be assessed for effects elsewhere.
  10. sustainment Turn 20. Sustainment concerns maintaining an accepted improvement through the agreed method and follow-up rather than a one-time result.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Waiting for shared tools, not slow operators

Fictional kaizen discussion: repeated waits for shared tools were observed, but the supervisor has described the operators as slow.

  1. Improvement facilitator: We observed repeated waiting for shared tools. Calling the operators slow doesn't describe that process problem.
  2. Production supervisor: What feedback would help the team without sounding like a personal criticism of my comment?
  3. Improvement facilitator: Say what happened: work paused while the tools were unavailable, which interrupted the observed flow.
  4. Production supervisor: Should we immediately buy another set and call the waste removed from the value stream?
  5. Improvement facilitator: We should examine the pattern and proposed change first. The observation doesn't establish the best solution.
  6. Production supervisor: I'll ask the team to show where the waiting occurs during the standard work.
  7. Improvement facilitator: Good. Invite their explanation of access and sequencing instead of assuming a motivation problem.
  8. Production supervisor: Then we can propose a small reviewed improvement and define what we will observe afterward.
  9. Improvement facilitator: Exactly. Kaizen feedback should connect the observed interruption to a practical next step.
  10. Production supervisor: I'll restate the issue as tool availability and keep any claim of improvement dependent on the follow-up evidence.
A long walk to the label printer

Separate fictional improvement case: an observer recorded six trips from packing to a shared label printer during one hour; distances and causes of repeat trips are unmeasured.

  1. Lean coordinator: I observed six trips to the label printer in one hour, interrupting the packing sequence.
  2. Packing supervisor: Are you saying the packers are walking too slowly between their tasks?
  3. Lean coordinator: No. My feedback concerns repeated travel in the process, not the speed or effort of individuals.
  4. Packing supervisor: Could we call moving the printer closer a confirmed time-saving change right away?
  5. Lean coordinator: It is an option to assess. We haven't measured distance, reasons for repeat trips, or the resulting time.
  6. Packing supervisor: I'll ask the packers which trips are necessary and where the standard work creates additional travel.
  7. Lean coordinator: Please use concrete examples so we can distinguish needed movement from possible waste.
  8. Packing supervisor: Then we'll discuss an appropriately reviewed layout trial and a clear comparison method.
  9. Lean coordinator: Good. Keep the observation separate from a promised improvement percentage.
  10. Packing supervisor: I'll bring the team's explanation to the kaizen discussion and agree how to evaluate any proposed change.
A handoff interrupts standard work

Separate fictional kaizen observation: an operator stopped the packing sequence twice to answer status questions already shown on the team's board.

  1. Team leader: I interrupted packing twice to ask for status that was already on the board.
  2. Improvement coach: That's a specific observation. What effect did those interruptions have on the standard-work sequence?
  3. Team leader: The operator stopped the sequence to answer me; I didn't measure the total delay.
  4. Improvement coach: Then describe the interruption without inventing a time loss or blaming the operator.
  5. Team leader: Could I try checking the board first and only ask when the status needs clarification?
  6. Improvement coach: That is a practical behavior to test, provided the board is kept current.
  7. Team leader: We haven't checked its update reliability yet, so I'll ask the team about that dependency.
  8. Improvement coach: Good. Continuous improvement should consider the information process as well as the individual interaction.
  9. Team leader: I'll explain what I observed and agree a trial for the next daily-management cycle.
  10. Improvement coach: Then review whether interruptions changed, without assuming the proposed habit eliminates all waste in the value stream.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The queue is for a shared tool
  1. Read the tool-waiting discussion. Say eight episodes and twenty-four operator-minutes within the one-hour observation.
  2. Swap roles. Contrast accumulated people-time with line downtime; keep the one-bench trial proposed and controls unchanged.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Add people-time, not clock-time

Four overlapping waiting episodes total 12 operator-minutes in a 30-minute observation. A new tool location is proposed, but no trial has run.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Facilitator: "The accumulated waiting measure is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. operator-minutes

The case adds individuals' waiting time, including overlapping episodes.

2. Supervisor: "Average waiting per episode is ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

A. three

Twelve operator-minutes divided by four episodes gives three per episode.

3. Facilitator: "The proposed tool location still needs a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. trial

No evaluation of the proposed location has yet been performed.

4. Supervisor: "We cannot report achieved ___ yet."

Answer and explanation

A. savings

A proposal has no measured improvement result before it is tested.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 02: Lean, Waste, and Continuous Improvement
Original fictional case: Improvement facilitator Ana and supervisor Marcus observe a one-hour assembly session. Eight waiting episodes for a shared tool total 24 operator-minutes; some episodes overlap, so this is not 24 minutes of line downtime. The supervisor calls the operators slow. The team proposes a one-bench point-of-use tool trial, but no trial result exists. Ana and Marcus must distinguish waiting from working pace, explain the time basis, and keep safety and quality checks unchanged in the proposal.
Language workshop: The queue is for a shared tool
Communication goal: Describe observed waiting, challenge unsupported blame, and define a bounded improvement trial without claiming savings in advance.
Language guidance: Twenty-four operator-minutes across eight episodes averages three operator-minutes per episode. Overlapping waits mean that total is not automatically elapsed line downtime. State the observation period and time basis when reporting the result.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Operators waited for the shared tool during eight observed episodes. / The total is 24 operator-minutes, not 24 minutes of line downtime. / Waiting for a tool does not by itself show slow working pace. / The observation does not establish that operator speed caused the delay. / Ask the operators where the tool is needed and what constrains access. / Record the present locations, demand, and waiting pattern. / Trial point-of-use storage at one bench. / No improvement result is available yet. / Required safety and quality checks remain unchanged. / Use the same time basis and comparable task conditions. / Confirm that the change does not create a shortage at another bench. / A one-bench test is not a factory-wide implementation. / Do not report proposed time savings as already achieved. / Review the waiting observations and any new difficulties after the trial. / Include the people who use the tool in the practical review. / Agree how an accepted improvement would be maintained.
Vocabulary: lean manufacturing: An approach to improving customer value by reducing waste in the process. / waste: Resource use that does not add the defined value or support a necessary requirement. / waiting: Time when work cannot proceed because a needed input is unavailable. / motion: Movement of people within a task or process. / transportation: Movement of material between locations or steps. / overproduction: Producing earlier or in greater quantity than needed. / overprocessing: Performing more work than the relevant requirement needs. / excess inventory: Material held beyond the justified operating need. / kaizen: Focused, ongoing improvement of work processes. / gemba: The actual place where the work occurs. / value stream: The connected activities and information involved in delivering a product. / current-state map: A representation of how the process currently works. / future-state map: A proposed representation of an improved process. / point-of-use storage: Keeping needed items near where they are used. / 5S: Workplace organization through sort, set in order, shine, standardize, and sustain. / standard work: The agreed method and conditions for performing a process. / pull system: Replenishment or production triggered by downstream need. / kanban: A signal authorizing defined replenishment or production in a pull system. / work-cell layout: The arrangement of people, equipment, and material in a production cell. / operator-minute: One minute of one operator's time, accumulated across people if specified. / baseline observation: A measurement of the current process before a proposed change. / pilot trial: A limited test of a proposed change before broader adoption. / countermeasure: An action intended to address an identified problem. / sustainment: Maintaining an improvement over time through defined follow-up.
Speaking task: Read the tool-waiting discussion. Say eight episodes and twenty-four operator-minutes within the one-hour observation. Swap roles. Contrast accumulated people-time with line downtime; keep the one-bench trial proposed and controls unchanged. Read the corrected four-episode transfer. State three operator-minutes per episode, with no achieved savings yet.
Partner: Ana: Improvement facilitator / Marcus: Assembly supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Four overlapping waiting episodes total 12 operator-minutes in a 30-minute observation. A new tool location is proposed, but no trial has run.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Marcus: After the trial, we should ask whether a reduction here created a tool shortage elsewhere. A faster bench would not necessarily mean a better overall flow.
Ana: Review the broader value stream. Local gains matter, but a change that simply transfers waiting to another step may not improve delivery to the customer.
Marcus: If the trial supports the change, the team still needs an approved method and follow-up. Otherwise the tool could gradually return to the old shared location.
Ana: That is sustainment. Update the agreed method through the proper process, clarify ownership, and monitor whether the improvement persists without weakening the required controls.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
