Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A rush order overlaps planned maintenance

Fictional scheduling negotiation: a rush order overlaps a planned maintenance window; no authorization to defer maintenance or spare capacity is confirmed.

  1. Production planner: The rush order overlaps the maintenance window. Could we postpone preventive maintenance to finish the order?
  2. Maintenance lead: We need the authorized assessment first. I can't assume deferral is acceptable for this equipment.
  3. Production planner: What options can we bring to the decision maker without overriding that boundary?
  4. Maintenance lead: Assess the order timing, any verified alternate capacity, and the maintenance team's permitted scheduling options.
  5. Production planner: Could we promise another line while its capacity and changeover requirements are still unknown?
  6. Maintenance lead: No. Present that as an option requiring confirmation, not available capacity.
  7. Production planner: I'll ask sales which delivery flexibility exists and keep the maintenance dependency explicit.
  8. Maintenance lead: I'll request the authorized timing assessment and identify any spare-parts dependency affecting the window.
  9. Production planner: Then we'll compare confirmed options before offering the customer a revised commitment.
  10. Maintenance lead: Agreed. We can negotiate the plan while leaving equipment and safety decisions with the responsible specialists.
A spare part has an estimate, not a confirmed arrival

Separate fictional maintenance plan: a required spare part has an estimated Tuesday delivery; the carrier has not confirmed arrival, and the maintenance window is Wednesday.

  1. Maintenance scheduler: The spare part is estimated for Tuesday, ahead of Wednesday's window, but arrival isn't confirmed.
  2. Production manager: Can we commit to a Wednesday completion so I can promise Thursday output?
  3. Maintenance scheduler: That would depend on part receipt and the authorized maintenance assessment, neither settled by the estimate.
  4. Production manager: What conditional plan can we discuss with the team instead of leaving the whole schedule blank?
  5. Maintenance scheduler: Keep Wednesday as planned, subject to confirmed readiness, and agree when to review the part status.
  6. Production manager: Could a different part be substituted if the delivery slips by a day?
  7. Maintenance scheduler: Only through the appropriate technical approval; we shouldn't negotiate an unassessed substitution here.
  8. Production manager: I'll explain the dependency and request a delivery confirmation before the planning checkpoint.
  9. Maintenance scheduler: I'll coordinate readiness information and avoid treating a forecast arrival as a completed maintenance input.
  10. Production manager: Then we'll update output commitments from verified conditions rather than a chain of optimistic assumptions.
Changeover timing with an unconfirmed extra order

Separate fictional planning case: a changeover is planned after order A; sales proposes adding order B first, but B's quantity and due date are unconfirmed.

  1. Sales coordinator: Could you delay the changeover and fit order B after A before switching products?
  2. Production scheduler: We can assess that, but B's quantity and required date haven't been confirmed.
  3. Sales coordinator: Keeping the current setup sounds efficient. Can we agree now and settle quantity later?
  4. Production scheduler: The benefit and impact depend on quantity, capacity, and the next scheduled order.
  5. Sales coordinator: What information would let you offer a useful tradeoff at the planning meeting?
  6. Production scheduler: Confirm B's scope and timing, then we can compare the changeover sequence and downstream commitments.
  7. Sales coordinator: I'll ask whether partial delivery is acceptable without promising that the line can provide it.
  8. Production scheduler: Good. We'll also retain any maintenance constraints rather than treat sequence flexibility as unlimited.
  9. Sales coordinator: I'll return the confirmed request before asking for a revised production commitment.
  10. Production scheduler: I'll assess the options with the responsible team and state the conditions attached to any feasible plan.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A rush order meets the maintenance window
  1. Read the schedule discussion. Identify the 13:00-to-14:00 conflict within the two-hour maintenance window.
  2. Swap roles. Present the other line as conditional, and distinguish proposed deferral from authorization.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

An option still has conditions

Maintenance is planned from 10:00 to 12:00. An order needs the same equipment until 11:00. A second line is possible but its suitability is unconfirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Planner: "The schedules overlap by ___ hour."

Answer and explanation

A. one

The shared period is 10:00 to 11:00, a one-hour overlap.

2. Technician: "Moving maintenance would be a proposed ___."

Answer and explanation

C. deferral

Postponement is not approved merely because an order conflicts with the window.

3. Planner: "The second line is a capacity ___."

Answer and explanation

C. contingency

The alternative remains conditional until its suitability and readiness are confirmed.

4. Technician: "Before using it, confirm its ___."

Answer and explanation

C. suitability

The briefing specifically leaves the second line's capability for the job unconfirmed.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 05: Maintenance, Reliability, and Changeover
Original fictional case: Maintenance planner Leila and production scheduler Ben face a conflict. A planned two-hour maintenance window runs from 13:00 to 15:00, while a new rush order would need the same line through 14:00 under the current schedule. No technical assessment or authorization to defer maintenance is supplied. An alternative line might be available, but its suitability is unconfirmed. Leila and Ben must prepare options for the responsible decision maker without describing a possible workaround as an approved plan.
Language workshop: A rush order meets the maintenance window
Communication goal: Explain schedule conflicts, distinguish work types, and present conditional options without assuming authority to defer maintenance or restart equipment.
Language guidance: The conflict is one hour, from 13:00 to 14:00, but moving a two-hour maintenance task may have consequences beyond that overlap. Its duration does not establish that it can be shortened, split, or deferred without the required assessment.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The order needs the line until 14:00, while maintenance starts at 13:00. / A one-hour conflict does not mean a two-hour task can be completed in one hour. / We do not yet have a basis for deferring the maintenance. / The responsible decision maker needs the technical assessment before approving a change. / An alternative line may be possible if suitability and readiness are confirmed. / An idle line is not automatically qualified for this order. / This is maintenance, not merely a product changeover. / Do not remove required work just to fit the schedule. / Confirm the work scope, required parts, personnel, and dependencies. / Recent operation does not prove the task can be postponed without consequence. / The plan needs its conditions stated before we promise completion. / Finishing the work is not the same as authorized return to service. / The decision maker can also assess a revised delivery commitment. / Update the schedule only after the authorized choice is documented. / Tell affected teams which line and time assumptions changed. / Present the options and unresolved conditions together.
Vocabulary: preventive maintenance: Planned work intended to reduce the likelihood of equipment failure. / predictive maintenance: Maintenance informed by indicators of equipment condition or future failure. / corrective maintenance: Work performed to restore function after a fault is identified. / condition monitoring: Tracking indicators of equipment health over time. / maintenance window: A reserved period for planned maintenance activity. / work order: A controlled record authorizing and describing a defined maintenance task. / backlog: Approved or identified work that remains unfinished. / critical asset: Equipment whose failure has significant operational or other consequences. / reliability: The ability to perform the required function over a stated period under stated conditions. / maintainability: The ease and speed with which equipment can be maintained or restored. / mean time between failures: Average operating time between failures for the defined repairable population. / mean time to repair: Average repair time under the stated measurement definition. / failure mode: The way in which an item fails to perform its function. / wear indicator: A measured or observed sign of component deterioration. / spare-part readiness: Availability of the correct parts needed for the planned work. / changeover: Transitioning equipment from one product or setup to another. / setup time: The time required to prepare equipment for a specified job. / SMED: Single-minute exchange of die: a method for reducing changeover time. / internal setup: Setup activity that requires the machine to be stopped. / external setup: Setup activity that can be completed while the machine is running. / deferral: Postponement of a planned task subject to the required assessment and decision. / return to service: The authorized restoration of equipment to operational use. / capacity contingency: A conditional alternative for meeting production needs. / schedule dependency: A condition or predecessor affecting the timing of planned work.
Speaking task: Read the schedule discussion. Identify the 13:00-to-14:00 conflict within the two-hour maintenance window. Swap roles. Present the other line as conditional, and distinguish proposed deferral from authorization. Read the corrected morning transfer. Preserve the one-hour overlap and the alternative line's unconfirmed suitability.
Partner: Leila: Maintenance planner / Ben: Production scheduler
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Maintenance is planned from 10:00 to 12:00. An order needs the same equipment until 11:00. A second line is possible but its suitability is unconfirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Our options are therefore conditional: assess the alternative line, assess a permitted schedule change, or discuss a revised delivery commitment. None is confirmed yet.
Leila: Yes. Show each schedule dependency and the responsible owner. That makes the trade-off visible without allowing the rush order to become an unsupported technical authorization.
Ben: Once the authorized choice is recorded, I will update the affected teams. I will also avoid describing the equipment as ready merely because the work is reported finished.
Leila: Good. Return to service remains a distinct status. The final update should identify the completed work, required checks, and actual authorization before making a production commitment.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06EHS and Safety CommunicationStop at the unclear instruction+

Your goal

Raise an ambiguous safety instruction clearly, distinguish shutdown from energy control, and confirm the authorized clarification route.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Stop at the unclear instruction

During a classroom safety exercise, operator Hana reads a training card saying switch off, then clean. Trainer Oscar confirms that the card does not identify the equipment, energy sources, or applicable isolation procedure. No actual equipment work is underway. Hana is not being authorized to service a machine. The exercise must stop at the ambiguous instruction and refer to the current approved equipment-specific procedure and qualified personnel. Hana and Oscar must clarify the communication without inventing an isolation sequence.

Hana: Operator in a classroom exercise / Oscar: Safety trainer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Where is this exchange taking place?

Answer and explanation

A. In a classroom safety exercise with no equipment work underway

The brief explicitly describes a classroom exercise and supplies no live-work authorization.

2. Which clarification is needed before this classroom card could support the intended technical discussion?

Answer and explanation

B. Identified equipment, energy sources, and the current applicable approved procedure

The card lacks the equipment-specific basis needed to interpret it. A signature or general statement does not supply that basis, and the exercise authorizes no actual work.

3. What must not be invented?

Answer and explanation

A. An isolation sequence for an unidentified machine

The task is to stop and clarify communication, not create a machine procedure without the required basis.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Stopping normal operation is not the same as controlling every hazardous energy source. This classroom card does not identify a machine or an approved method. The correct language response is to pause and seek the applicable procedure, not supply missing operational steps.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
EHS
Environment, health, and safety functions and practices.raise an EHS concern
hazard
A source or condition with the potential to cause harm.identify a hazard
hazardous energy
Energy that can injure someone if unexpectedly released or applied.recognize hazardous energy
energy isolation
Separation from energy sources using the applicable control method.follow the energy-isolation procedure
lockout
Securing an energy-isolating device in the required safe position under the applicable procedure.follow the lockout procedure
tagout
Use of a warning tag within an applicable energy-control system.understand tagout limitations
stored energy
Energy remaining in a system after its normal supply is interrupted.address stored energy
residual energy
Energy that remains after an initial control or shutdown action.assess residual energy
shutdown
Stopping normal equipment operation.distinguish shutdown from isolation
unexpected startup
Unintended equipment operation that can expose people to hazards.prevent unexpected startup
authorized employee
A person assigned and trained for the relevant hazardous-energy-control work.identify the authorized employee
affected employee
A person whose operation or work area is affected by energy-control activity.inform affected employees
equipment-specific procedure
Approved instructions applicable to identified equipment and work.consult the equipment-specific procedure
verification of isolation
Confirmation of the required isolated state using the approved method.require verification of isolation
stop-work concern
A safety concern requiring work to pause under the applicable process.raise a stop-work concern
near miss
An event that could have caused harm but did not in the observed outcome.report a near miss
job hazard analysis
Review of job steps, associated hazards, and appropriate controls.complete a job hazard analysis
permit to work
A formal authorization process for defined work where required.verify permit-to-work requirements
personal protective equipment
Protective items worn to reduce exposure to specified hazards.select required personal protective equipment
hierarchy of controls
An ordered approach to choosing hazard-control measures.apply the hierarchy of controls
safety data sheet
A document describing a chemical's hazards and handling information.consult the safety data sheet
exclusion zone
An area with controlled access because of defined hazards or work.respect the exclusion zone
read-back
Repeating critical information to check shared understanding.request a read-back
restart authorization
The required permission to resume equipment operation.confirm restart authorization

A tag and a physical lock do not perform identical functions. Training participation does not by itself authorize a person for a particular task. Apply the actual energy-control program and assigned roles; no sentence in this exercise permits servicing or restart.

C Notice the language

A short stop message can be respectful

Safety clarification works best when the concern is specific and the pause is unambiguous. Say which instruction is unclear and what needs confirmation. Do not bury the concern in an apology or turn uncertainty into a request to guess the missing step.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the concern
Stop here: the instruction does not identify the equipment or isolation procedure.
Keep the pause clear
We should not continue from this ambiguous card.
Distinguish the actions
Switching off is not the same as verifying energy isolation.
Identify missing context
Which equipment and approved procedure does this instruction refer to?
Request the controlled source
Use the current equipment-specific procedure.
Respect assigned roles
The relevant authorized personnel must handle the required assessment and work.
Avoid improvisation
I will not infer the missing sequence from a generic instruction.
Keep training separate
This classroom exercise does not authorize actual servicing.
Ask for confirmation
Please confirm the document identity and current revision.
Use read-back
I will repeat the clarified scope and next step to check understanding.
Avoid a false safe claim
We have no evidence here that any equipment is isolated.
Do not substitute protection
Protective equipment does not replace the required energy-control process.
Name the escalation
Oscar will refer the ambiguous card for correction through the training process.
Preserve the issue
Record the unclear wording so it is not reused unchanged.
Separate restart
A clarification does not itself authorize equipment restart.
Close the exercise
We will resume the classroom task only with the appropriate reviewed material.
6 language and precision notes
Stop
A clear pause instruction; do not dilute it with several uncertain alternatives.
Off
May describe a control state without establishing isolation.
Safe
A broad conclusion that needs a defined basis and scope.
Authorized
Refers to assigned role and required competence, not mere presence.
Verify
Requires the approved method; it is not a guess or visual assumption.
Read back
Confirms shared understanding but does not replace technical verification.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best fits the ambiguous card?

Answer and explanation

B. Pause and obtain the current applicable equipment-specific procedure through the authorized route.

The missing context requires clarification by the proper process, not invented operational instructions.

2. What does switching off establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

C. Normal operation may be stopped; complete energy isolation is not established.

Shutdown and complete hazardous-energy control are distinct, so the broader conclusion is unsupported.

3. What can read-back confirm?

Answer and explanation

B. Shared understanding of the clarified message, not the physical isolated state

Repeating information checks communication and does not replace technical verification or role requirements.

4. Which closing statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. The ambiguous classroom card is referred for correction; no equipment work or restart is authorized.

The exercise identifies a communication problem without supplying live-work or restart authorization.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Stop at the unclear instruction

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hana: Operator in a classroom exercise / Oscar: Safety trainer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • read-back
  • equipment-specific procedure
  • restart authorization
  • Verification of isolation
  • Stored energy
  • Tagout
  • Shutdown
  • EHS
  • stop-work concern
  • authorized employee
  1. Hana
    I want to stop at this line. The card says switch off, then clean, but it does not identify the machine or the procedure we should be using.
  2. Oscar
    That is a clear 1. We are in a classroom exercise, and we should not continue as though the missing equipment information were already known.
  3. Hana
    The phrase switch off sounds simple, but it could refer only to stopping normal operation. It does not tell me what happens to other energy sources.
  4. Oscar
    Correct. 2 is not the same as complete energy control. This card gives us no basis for declaring any machine isolated or safe for the proposed work.
  5. Hana
    There could also be energy remaining after a normal stop. I should not assume that the absence of movement proves there is nothing left that could cause harm.
  6. Oscar
    Exactly. 3 must be addressed through the applicable process. We will not improvise that process for unidentified equipment during a language exercise.
  7. Hana
    Which machine is this card supposed to cover? Can we get its approved instructions before the class goes any further?
  8. Oscar
    We need the current 4 and the relevant qualified personnel. The correct document and assigned roles matter as much as the words on the card.
  9. Hana
    I understand the need to ask, but I do not want my question to sound as though I am volunteering to perform the isolation myself.
  10. Oscar
    You are not. The 5 has an assigned role and the required training. Participating in this discussion does not give you that authorization.
  11. Hana
    Some learners also treat a tag as though it physically prevents the same actions as a lock. We should make the distinction clear without demonstrating an unapproved method.
  12. Oscar
    Yes. 6 has particular functions and limitations under the applicable system. A warning tag must not be described as providing identical physical restraint to a lock.
  13. Hana
    Once the correct information is available, I can repeat the equipment identity and the next communication step. That would help confirm that I heard the clarification correctly.
  14. Oscar
    That is 7. It checks our shared understanding; it does not replace the required technical process or demonstrate that the equipment has reached a particular state.
  15. Hana
    The worksheet asks me to mark the machine isolated. I am leaving that blank: we are in a classroom and have not checked any equipment.
  16. Oscar
    Correct. 8 is a separate technical requirement under the applicable procedure. Our conversation does not establish that it has occurred.
  17. Hana
    I will keep the concern specific: missing equipment identity and procedure. The training card needs correction, rather than a handwritten guess about the missing steps.
  18. Oscar
    I will refer it through the 9 and training process. We should preserve the unclear wording so the responsible people can review and correct the source.
  19. Hana
    Then we pause this part of the exercise until the reviewed material is available. No one should interpret the clarification as permission to start cleaning a machine.
  20. Oscar
    Exactly. It also provides no 10. The outcome today is a clear concern, a proper referral, and an explicit boundary between classroom communication and actual equipment work.

Answers and explanations
  1. stop-work concern Turn 2. The concern calls for a pause at the ambiguous instruction rather than guessing a work sequence.
  2. Shutdown Turn 4. Shutdown stops normal operation but does not establish that every hazardous energy source has been controlled.
  3. Stored energy Turn 6. Stored energy can remain after normal supply or operation stops, so it cannot be dismissed from a generic off instruction.
  4. equipment-specific procedure Turn 8. The equipment-specific procedure supplies the approved instructions for identified equipment rather than a generic classroom shortcut.
  5. authorized employee Turn 10. The authorized employee has the assigned role and required training for the relevant energy-control work.
  6. Tagout Turn 12. Tagout uses warning tags within the applicable system and must not be assumed to provide the same physical restraint as a lock.
  7. read-back Turn 14. Read-back checks shared understanding of critical information but does not establish a physical equipment condition.
  8. Verification of isolation Turn 16. Verification of isolation requires the approved technical method, which has not occurred in this classroom discussion.
  9. EHS Turn 18. EHS identifies the environment, health, and safety function involved in addressing the safety-related training issue.
  10. restart authorization Turn 20. Restart authorization is a separate required permission and is not created by completing a classroom clarification.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: I want to stop at this line. The card says switch off, then clean, but it does not identify the machine or the procedure we should be using.
  2. Oscar: That is a clear stop-work concern. We are in a classroom exercise, and we should not continue as though the missing equipment information were already known.
  3. Hana: The phrase switch off sounds simple, but it could refer only to stopping normal operation. It does not tell me what happens to other energy sources.
  4. Oscar: Correct. Shutdown is not the same as complete energy control. This card gives us no basis for declaring any machine isolated or safe for the proposed work.
  5. Hana: There could also be energy remaining after a normal stop. I should not assume that the absence of movement proves there is nothing left that could cause harm.
  6. Oscar: Exactly. Stored energy must be addressed through the applicable process. We will not improvise that process for unidentified equipment during a language exercise.
  7. Hana: Which machine is this card supposed to cover? Can we get its approved instructions before the class goes any further?
  8. Oscar: We need the current equipment-specific procedure and the relevant qualified personnel. The correct document and assigned roles matter as much as the words on the card.
  9. Hana: I understand the need to ask, but I do not want my question to sound as though I am volunteering to perform the isolation myself.
  10. Oscar: You are not. The authorized employee has an assigned role and the required training. Participating in this discussion does not give you that authorization.
  11. Hana: Some learners also treat a tag as though it physically prevents the same actions as a lock. We should make the distinction clear without demonstrating an unapproved method.
  12. Oscar: Yes. Tagout has particular functions and limitations under the applicable system. A warning tag must not be described as providing identical physical restraint to a lock.
  13. Hana: Once the correct information is available, I can repeat the equipment identity and the next communication step. That would help confirm that I heard the clarification correctly.
  14. Oscar: That is read-back. It checks our shared understanding; it does not replace the required technical process or demonstrate that the equipment has reached a particular state.
  15. Hana: The worksheet asks me to mark the machine isolated. I am leaving that blank: we are in a classroom and have not checked any equipment.
  16. Oscar: Correct. Verification of isolation is a separate technical requirement under the applicable procedure. Our conversation does not establish that it has occurred.
  17. Hana: I will keep the concern specific: missing equipment identity and procedure. The training card needs correction, rather than a handwritten guess about the missing steps.
  18. Oscar: I will refer it through the EHS and training process. We should preserve the unclear wording so the responsible people can review and correct the source.
  19. Hana: Then we pause this part of the exercise until the reviewed material is available. No one should interpret the clarification as permission to start cleaning a machine.
  20. Oscar: Exactly. It also provides no restart authorization. The outcome today is a clear concern, a proper referral, and an explicit boundary between classroom communication and actual equipment work.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An unclear isolation instruction before work begins

Fictional safety-communication exercise: an equipment-isolation instruction is ambiguous, no work has begun, and the local approved procedure must govern the task.

  1. Maintenance technician: This isolation instruction is unclear. I need the authorized supervisor to clarify it before the task proceeds.
  2. Safety supervisor: Which wording is ambiguous, and have you identified the approved procedure for this equipment?
  3. Maintenance technician: The instruction doesn't identify the equipment reference clearly. I won't guess the intended machine.
  4. Safety supervisor: Good. We need the correct reference and applicable lockout/tagout procedure, not an improvised interpretation.
  5. Maintenance technician: Can you take the clarification request and confirm who is authorized to resolve it?
  6. Safety supervisor: Yes. I'll coordinate that check through our local safety process.
  7. Maintenance technician: Should I treat a coworker's memory as a substitute while the document is checked?
  8. Safety supervisor: No. This exercise calls for clarification through the approved route before proceeding, not operational shortcuts.
  9. Maintenance technician: I'll keep the task pending and provide the exact instruction reference for review.
  10. Safety supervisor: Request received. I'll return the verified clarification and keep equipment decisions with the authorized team.
A near-miss report needs the location clarified

Separate fictional EHS reporting exercise: a near miss is reported near a loading area, but the exact location is missing and no injury is reported in the supplied account.

  1. Warehouse supervisor: The report says near the loading area. Could you clarify the location before we route the follow-up?
  2. EHS coordinator: I'll request the specific location from the reporter and preserve the current account as incomplete.
  3. Warehouse supervisor: Can we enter bay four because it is the busiest bay on this shift?
  4. EHS coordinator: No. That would invent an event detail and could send the review to the wrong area.
  5. Warehouse supervisor: Please also confirm which responsible team should receive the report through our safety process.
  6. EHS coordinator: I will. The absence of an injury in the account doesn't remove the need for the reporting review.
  7. Warehouse supervisor: Should the briefing state that the area is now safe because the event ended?
  8. EHS coordinator: No assessment supporting that assurance is supplied here. Keep the known event separate from current safety status.
  9. Warehouse supervisor: I'll request a verified location and avoid adding a cause or safety conclusion.
  10. EHS coordinator: I'll acknowledge the handoff and arrange the appropriate follow-up under the local EHS process.
A stop-work concern needs a named receiver

Separate fictional safety drill: a technician raises a stop-work concern about conflicting task instructions, but no supervisor has acknowledged it; no operating steps are supplied.

  1. Technician: I've raised a stop-work concern about conflicting instructions, but I haven't received an acknowledgment.
  2. Shift supervisor: Please state the conflict clearly so I can receive the concern and route it appropriately.
  3. Technician: The two task sheets identify different equipment references. I need confirmation of the applicable instruction.
  4. Shift supervisor: Understood. We'll use the local approved process to resolve the conflict before the task proceeds.
  5. Technician: Can you confirm that you're accepting the clarification request, not simply noting that I called?
  6. Shift supervisor: Yes. I'll coordinate the authorized review and confirm the responsible decision maker.
  7. Technician: A colleague wants to choose the sheet with the newer print date. Is that verification?
  8. Shift supervisor: Not by itself. A print date doesn't establish which approved instruction governs this task.
  9. Technician: I'll preserve both references and keep the conflict visible in the handoff.
  10. Shift supervisor: Good. We'll return a verified instruction through the proper route rather than settle an equipment-safety question by guesswork.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Stop at the unclear instruction
  1. Read the classroom exchange. Pause at the ambiguous card and request the identified equipment and applicable approved procedure.
  2. Swap roles. Distinguish shutdown, communication read-back, and physical isolation verification without inventing operating steps.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarification is not authorization

A classroom card omits the machine identity and approved procedure. No live equipment work has occurred. The trainer will obtain reviewed material.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Learner: "The equipment identity is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. missing

The card omits the specific machine, so its identity cannot be assumed.

2. Trainer: "We must obtain the applicable approved ___."

Answer and explanation

D. procedure

A generic card cannot replace the required equipment-specific instructions.

3. Learner: "This discussion remains a classroom ___."

Answer and explanation

B. exercise

The case supplies no actual equipment work or servicing authorization.

4. Trainer: "It does not authorize a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. restart

A training clarification does not provide permission to resume equipment operation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Manufacturing English
Professional audience: plant managers, production supervisors, quality engineers, maintenance teams, industrial engineers, safety leads, supply planners, and manufacturing-adjacent professionals
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not equipment-operation, maintenance, engineering, or safety instructions. Figures, organizations, and workplace cases are invented. Actual work must follow applicable law, approved site procedures, trained personnel, and authorized decisions. Classroom safety discussions do not authorize isolation, servicing, or restart.
Lesson 06: EHS and Safety Communication
Original fictional case: During a classroom safety exercise, operator Hana reads a training card saying switch off, then clean. Trainer Oscar confirms that the card does not identify the equipment, energy sources, or applicable isolation procedure. No actual equipment work is underway. Hana is not being authorized to service a machine. The exercise must stop at the ambiguous instruction and refer to the current approved equipment-specific procedure and qualified personnel. Hana and Oscar must clarify the communication without inventing an isolation sequence.
Language workshop: Stop at the unclear instruction
Communication goal: Raise an ambiguous safety instruction clearly, distinguish shutdown from energy control, and confirm the authorized clarification route.
Language guidance: Stopping normal operation is not the same as controlling every hazardous energy source. This classroom card does not identify a machine or an approved method. The correct language response is to pause and seek the applicable procedure, not supply missing operational steps.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Stop here: the instruction does not identify the equipment or isolation procedure. / We should not continue from this ambiguous card. / Switching off is not the same as verifying energy isolation. / Which equipment and approved procedure does this instruction refer to? / Use the current equipment-specific procedure. / The relevant authorized personnel must handle the required assessment and work. / I will not infer the missing sequence from a generic instruction. / This classroom exercise does not authorize actual servicing. / Please confirm the document identity and current revision. / I will repeat the clarified scope and next step to check understanding. / We have no evidence here that any equipment is isolated. / Protective equipment does not replace the required energy-control process. / Oscar will refer the ambiguous card for correction through the training process. / Record the unclear wording so it is not reused unchanged. / A clarification does not itself authorize equipment restart. / We will resume the classroom task only with the appropriate reviewed material.
Vocabulary: EHS: Environment, health, and safety functions and practices. / hazard: A source or condition with the potential to cause harm. / hazardous energy: Energy that can injure someone if unexpectedly released or applied. / energy isolation: Separation from energy sources using the applicable control method. / lockout: Securing an energy-isolating device in the required safe position under the applicable procedure. / tagout: Use of a warning tag within an applicable energy-control system. / stored energy: Energy remaining in a system after its normal supply is interrupted. / residual energy: Energy that remains after an initial control or shutdown action. / shutdown: Stopping normal equipment operation. / unexpected startup: Unintended equipment operation that can expose people to hazards. / authorized employee: A person assigned and trained for the relevant hazardous-energy-control work. / affected employee: A person whose operation or work area is affected by energy-control activity. / equipment-specific procedure: Approved instructions applicable to identified equipment and work. / verification of isolation: Confirmation of the required isolated state using the approved method. / stop-work concern: A safety concern requiring work to pause under the applicable process. / near miss: An event that could have caused harm but did not in the observed outcome. / job hazard analysis: Review of job steps, associated hazards, and appropriate controls. / permit to work: A formal authorization process for defined work where required. / personal protective equipment: Protective items worn to reduce exposure to specified hazards. / hierarchy of controls: An ordered approach to choosing hazard-control measures. / safety data sheet: A document describing a chemical's hazards and handling information. / exclusion zone: An area with controlled access because of defined hazards or work. / read-back: Repeating critical information to check shared understanding. / restart authorization: The required permission to resume equipment operation.
Speaking task: Read the classroom exchange. Pause at the ambiguous card and request the identified equipment and applicable approved procedure. Swap roles. Distinguish shutdown, communication read-back, and physical isolation verification without inventing operating steps. Read the corrected transfer. Keep the activity a classroom exercise with no servicing or restart authorization.
Partner: Hana: Operator in a classroom exercise / Oscar: Safety trainer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A classroom card omits the machine identity and approved procedure. No live equipment work has occurred. The trainer will obtain reviewed material.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hana: I will keep the concern specific: missing equipment identity and procedure. The training card needs correction, rather than a handwritten guess about the missing steps.
Oscar: I will refer it through the EHS and training process. We should preserve the unclear wording so the responsible people can review and correct the source.
Hana: Then we pause this part of the exercise until the reviewed material is available. No one should interpret the clarification as permission to start cleaning a machine.
Oscar: Exactly. It also provides no restart authorization. The outcome today is a clear concern, a proper referral, and an explicit boundary between classroom communication and actual equipment work.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Hana: Then we pause this part of the exercise until the reviewed material is available. No one should interpret the clarification as permission to start cleaning a machine.
Oscar: Exactly. It also provides no restart authorization. The outcome today is a clear concern, a proper referral, and an explicit boundary between classroom communication and actual equipment work.
END REFERENCE
07Supplier Quality and Incoming MaterialsOne dimension, two drawing revisions+

Your goal

Compare measured results against explicit revision limits and resolve the governing requirement before assigning responsibility or release status.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

One dimension, two drawing revisions

Supplier-quality engineer Farah and supplier contact Colin review a component measured at 12.18 mm. The incoming report omits the drawing revision. The inspector used revision C, specifying 12.00 plus or minus 0.10 mm; the supplier used revision B, specifying 12.20 plus or minus 0.10 mm. Which revision governs the order has not been confirmed. The lot is held pending review. Farah and Colin must state both comparisons accurately, trace the order documents, and avoid declaring either party responsible before resolving the requirement.

Farah: Supplier-quality engineer / Colin: Supplier quality contact

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the revision C range?

Answer and explanation

D. 11.90 to 12.10 mm

A nominal 12.00 mm with a tolerance of 0.10 gives limits of 11.90 and 12.10.

2. How does 12.18 mm compare with revision B's stated range?

Answer and explanation

A. It lies within 12.10 to 12.30 mm.

The measurement is within the stated B limits, but that comparison does not establish the governing revision.

3. What remains unconfirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. Which drawing revision governs this order

The brief supplies both references but not the agreed revision applicable to the order.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Revision B permits 12.10 to 12.30 mm; revision C permits 11.90 to 12.10 mm. The measured 12.18 mm is within the supplied B limits and 0.08 mm above C's upper limit. Neither comparison decides which revision governs the order.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
engineering drawing
A controlled technical representation of product requirements.consult the engineering drawing
drawing revision
The identified version of a technical drawing.confirm the drawing revision
nominal dimension
The stated target or reference size.identify the nominal dimension
tolerance
The permitted variation from a specified value.apply the stated tolerance
upper specification limit
The largest value permitted by the stated requirement.check the upper specification limit
lower specification limit
The smallest value permitted by the stated requirement.check the lower specification limit
measured value
The result reported by the measurement process.record the measured value
measurement unit
The scale used to express a measurement, such as millimeters.state the measurement unit
inspection characteristic
A specified feature selected for inspection.identify the inspection characteristic
inspection report
A record of examined features, methods, and results.correct the inspection report
purchase order
The commercial document defining the ordered goods and referenced requirements.review the purchase order
order acknowledgment
The supplier's confirmation of the order terms it received or accepted.compare the order acknowledgment
applicable revision
The document version governing the specified work or order.establish the applicable revision
document discrepancy
A mismatch between relevant records or references.resolve a document discrepancy
controlled copy
A document copy maintained under the applicable version-control process.obtain the controlled copy
superseded revision
A version replaced by a later one in the relevant document system.identify a superseded revision
engineering change notice
A controlled communication describing an approved technical change.trace the engineering change notice
effectivity
The point or population from which a change applies.confirm change effectivity
supplier flow-down
Communication of applicable requirements through the supply chain.verify supplier flow-down
receiving record
Documentation of material received and its identifying details.trace the receiving record
lot identity
The identifier linking material to a specific batch.preserve lot identity
acceptance basis
The requirements and evidence used for an acceptance decision.state the acceptance basis
deviation request
A request for authorized departure from a specified requirement.review a deviation request
release decision
The authorized determination that material may proceed to use or distribution.document the release decision

Latest in a drawing system and applicable to this order are not necessarily identical. Review the order, acknowledgment, change communication, and effectivity. A numerical pass against one reference does not itself authorize release when the governing requirement remains unresolved.

C Notice the language

Resolve the reference before arguing about compliance

Two people can make internally consistent statements while using different documents. Put the requirement identity and revision beside the result, then trace the agreed order. This avoids treating a reference mismatch as dishonesty and keeps the discussion focused on evidence and responsibility.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the result fully
The reported dimension is 12.18 millimeters.
Name the missing reference
The inspection report does not identify the drawing revision.
Show both bases
The inspector used C; the supplier used B.
Read the C limits
Revision C permits 11.90 to 12.10 millimeters.
Read the B limits
Revision B permits 12.10 to 12.30 millimeters.
Report the comparison
The result is within B's stated limits and above C's upper limit.
Avoid premature blame
We have not established which revision governs the order.
Trace the agreement
Compare the purchase order with the supplier acknowledgment.
Check the change timing
Confirm when revision C became applicable to this order or lot.
Preserve the original record
Add the corrected reference through the controlled process.
Keep the hold visible
The lot remains held pending the required review.
Separate dimension and release
Meeting one dimensional range is not the complete release decision.
Request the controlled source
Send the referenced drawing and relevant change communication.
Clarify a departure
A deviation request is not already an approved exception.
Record the resolution
Document the applicable revision and the resulting assessment.
Close the supplier exchange
Resolve the requirement basis before assigning responsibility or promising release.
6 language and precision notes
Within
Names a comparison to defined limits; it does not identify which limits apply.
Latest
Describes version chronology, not necessarily contractual effectivity.
Applicable
Connects a requirement to the particular work, order, or lot.
Plus or minus
Sets symmetric limits around the stated nominal value.
Acknowledged
Records the supplier's response; compare it with the actual order terms.
Released
Requires the relevant authorization, not merely a favorable number.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What is the difference between 12.18 mm and revision C's upper limit?

Answer and explanation

B. 0.08 mm above the limit

The upper limit is 12.10 mm, and 12.18 minus 12.10 equals 0.08 mm.

2. Which update keeps the numerical result separate from the unresolved governing requirement?

Answer and explanation

B. 12.18 meets B's 12.10-12.30 range and exceeds C's upper limit by 0.08; applicability and release remain open.

C's upper limit is 12.10, making the excess 0.08, not the distance from nominal. Neither numerical fit nor revision chronology establishes applicability or release authority.

3. Which evidence best addresses the mismatch?

Answer and explanation

B. Order references, acknowledgment, drawing versions, and change effectivity records

The relevant records connect the agreed requirement and change timing to the particular order and lot.

4. How should the report be corrected?

Answer and explanation

C. Add the missing revision and resolution through the controlled record process while retaining traceability.

The correction must preserve the measured result and traceable requirement basis rather than obscure the discrepancy.

One dimension, two drawing revisions

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Farah: Supplier-quality engineer / Colin: Supplier quality contact

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • drawing revision

  1. Colin
    We measured 12.18 millimeters and reported it within tolerance. Your incoming report says it failed. Can we compare the requirement before we debate the result?
  2. Farah
    Yes. The report is missing the 1. Your team used B, our inspector used C, and we have not confirmed which governs this order.
  3. Colin
    B says nominal 12.20, plus or minus 0.10 millimeters. We used that drawing in production, but I agree its applicability still needs checking.
  4. Farah
    B's 2 is 12.10 millimeters, and its upper limit is 12.30. The measured 12.18 is inside that interval.
  5. Colin
    C says 12.00, plus or minus 0.10. The upper end looks like the lower end of B, which may be why the report became confusing.
  6. Farah
    C's 3 is 12.10. The measurement is 0.08 above it, with C's full range running from 11.90 to 12.10.
  7. Colin
    Then neither team needs a different measurement to explain the disagreement. The same 12.18 gets a different comparison because the references differ.
  8. Farah
    Keep the 4 unchanged. We need to resolve the reference, not adjust the recorded number until it fits a preferred drawing.
  9. Colin
    Our production team says B came with the order. I will send the actual package rather than ask you to accept that recollection.
  10. Farah
    Include the 5 and attached drawing. I will compare them with our purchase order and the controlled requirement references.
  11. Colin
    I found a later change notice too. Its issue date does not tell me whether it applied to material already ordered.
  12. Farah
    Check its 6: the order, lot, date, or other stated application point. Newest in the system does not necessarily mean governing this lot.
  13. Colin
    If C was meant to apply, we also need to know whether the requirement reached our team. I will trace the communication history.
  14. Farah
    That is the 7 question. Keep it evidence-based; a mismatch does not yet tell us which party caused it.
  15. Colin
    Production wants a release estimate. Can I say the lot is usable because 12.18 meets B, while we finish the paperwork?
  16. Farah
    No. The 8 is unresolved and the lot is held. A favorable comparison with B does not supply the missing authorization.
  17. Colin
    We could request a formal exception if one is needed. I will keep that separate from asking which revision actually governed the order.
  18. Farah
    Correct. A 9 is a request, not an approved departure. Its submission would not itself remove the hold.
  19. Colin
    My update will show both ranges, the unchanged measurement, and the documents we are tracing. I will not promise use of the lot.
  20. Farah
    Once the requirement is resolved, record the authorized 10 and correct the report transparently. Preserve the measurement, references, and history of the discrepancy.

Answers and explanations
  1. drawing revision Turn 2. The drawing revision identifies the requirement version needed to interpret the inspection result.
  2. lower specification limit Turn 4. Subtracting 0.10 from the B nominal 12.20 gives the lower permitted value of 12.10 mm.
  3. upper specification limit Turn 6. Adding 0.10 to the C nominal 12.00 gives the upper permitted value of 12.10 mm.
  4. measured value Turn 8. The measured value remains 12.18 mm; differing reference limits change its interpretation, not the recorded number.
  5. order acknowledgment Turn 10. The acknowledgment records the supplier's received or accepted terms and can be compared with the purchase order references.
  6. effectivity Turn 12. Effectivity specifies when or to which population the technical change applies, resolving more than simple revision chronology.
  7. supplier flow-down Turn 14. Supplier flow-down concerns how applicable requirements were communicated through the supply chain.
  8. acceptance basis Turn 16. The acceptance basis remains unresolved while the applicable revision is unknown, so a favorable B comparison cannot settle release.
  9. deviation request Turn 18. A deviation request seeks an authorized departure and is not itself approval of that departure.
  10. release decision Turn 20. The release decision is the authorized result of the review, not an assumption drawn from one favorable comparison.
Read the complete conversation

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An inspection dimension without a drawing revision

Fictional supplier-quality call: an incoming report cites a dimension but omits the drawing revision; the supplier used a different revision.

  1. Supplier quality engineer: The incoming report cites a dimension without the drawing revision. Which specification was the inspection based on?
  2. Supplier representative: Our team used a different revision from the one your inspector mentioned verbally.
  3. Supplier quality engineer: Before discussing a deviation, we need the controlling requirement and both source references confirmed.
  4. Supplier representative: Could we accept the newer-looking drawing because it would resolve the disagreement quickly?
  5. Supplier quality engineer: Appearance or print date doesn't establish the controlling revision for this order.
  6. Supplier representative: I'll provide the drawing reference used for production and the order documentation available to us.
  7. Supplier quality engineer: I'll request the inspection source and verify the applicable specification through document control.
  8. Supplier representative: Should either team label the lot conforming or nonconforming before that clarification is complete?
  9. Supplier quality engineer: We shouldn't invent a conclusion from the conflicting references; disposition remains with the authorized quality process.
  10. Supplier representative: Agreed. We'll compare the verified requirement and measurement evidence before resolving the reported difference.
Millimeters or inches on the supplier report?

Separate fictional incoming inspection: a supplier report lists a dimension as 2.5 without a unit; the receiving specification uses millimeters.

  1. Incoming inspector: Your report lists two point five but no unit. Could you confirm the measurement unit used?
  2. Supplier technician: I need to check the original record rather than assume it matches your millimeter specification.
  3. Incoming inspector: Thank you. A bare number doesn't let us compare the result reliably with the requirement.
  4. Supplier technician: Could you convert it as inches while I look for the record, just to keep inspection moving?
  5. Incoming inspector: No. That would base the assessment on an unverified unit and could change the conclusion.
  6. Supplier technician: I'll retrieve the source measurement and ask for a corrected report with the unit stated.
  7. Incoming inspector: Please include the applicable specification reference and drawing revision in that clarification.
  8. Supplier technician: Understood. I won't describe the lot as accepted just because the numerical value looks familiar.
  9. Incoming inspector: I'll keep the comparison pending and pass the verified information to the responsible quality reviewer.
  10. Supplier technician: I'll return the source-backed correction so the decision rests on the actual measurement rather than a guessed conversion.
A deviation request is not an approved exception

Separate fictional supplier discussion: a supplier submitted a deviation request for lot M6, but the receiving team has no approval record.

  1. Buyer: The supplier sent a deviation request for M6. Does that mean the specification exception is approved?
  2. Supplier quality lead: No. We have a request, but no verified authorization for the exception.
  3. Buyer: Which question should I ask before I give production an update on the lot?
  4. Supplier quality lead: Ask for the review status, the precise scope requested, and the responsible approval record if one exists.
  5. Buyer: Could we assume approval applies to future lots as well if M6 eventually receives it?
  6. Supplier quality lead: Not without the actual authorized scope. A specific exception shouldn't become a blanket specification change.
  7. Buyer: I'll distinguish requested from approved and keep the M6 reference in every status message.
  8. Supplier quality lead: Please also avoid treating supplier acknowledgment as confirmation from our quality decision maker.
  9. Buyer: I'll request the verified status and leave the material commitment conditional.
  10. Supplier quality lead: I'll review the returned information through the appropriate process and clarify any limits before the status is communicated further.

Vocabulary: engineering drawing: A controlled technical representation of product requirements. / drawing revision: The identified version of a technical drawing. / nominal dimension: The stated target or reference size. / tolerance: The permitted variation from a specified value. / upper specification limit: The largest value permitted by the stated requirement. / lower specification limit: The smallest value permitted by the stated requirement. / measured value: The result reported by the measurement process. / measurement unit: The scale used to express a measurement, such as millimeters. / inspection characteristic: A specified feature selected for inspection. / inspection report: A record of examined features, methods, and results. / purchase order: The commercial document defining the ordered goods and referenced requirements. / order acknowledgment: The supplier's confirmation of the order terms it received or accepted. / applicable revision: The document version governing the specified work or order. / document discrepancy: A mismatch between relevant records or references. / controlled copy: A document copy maintained under the applicable version-control process. / superseded revision: A version replaced by a later one in the relevant document system. / engineering change notice: A controlled communication describing an approved technical change. / effectivity: The point or population from which a change applies. / supplier flow-down: Communication of applicable requirements through the supply chain. / receiving record: Documentation of material received and its identifying details. / lot identity: The identifier linking material to a specific batch. / acceptance basis: The requirements and evidence used for an acceptance decision. / deviation request: A request for authorized departure from a specified requirement. / release decision: The authorized determination that material may proceed to use or distribution.
Speaking task: Read the supplier exchange. Compare 12.18 with B's 12.10-12.30 and C's 11.90-12.10 millimeter ranges. Swap roles. State 0.08 above C's upper limit; preserve the measurement and the unresolved order effectivity. Read the corrected 5.08-millimeter transfer. It meets A and exceeds B; applicability is unconfirmed and the lot remains held.
Partner: Farah: Supplier-quality engineer / Colin: Supplier quality contact
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A measurement is 5.08 mm. Revision A allows 4.90-5.10 mm; revision B allows 4.95-5.05 mm. The governing revision is unconfirmed and the lot remains held.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Colin: We could request a formal exception if one is needed. I will keep that separate from asking which revision actually governed the order.
Farah: Correct. A deviation request is a request, not an approved departure. Its submission would not itself remove the hold.
Colin: My update will show both ranges, the unchanged measurement, and the documents we are tracing. I will not promise use of the lot.
Farah: Once the requirement is resolved, record the authorized release decision and correct the report transparently. Preserve the measurement, references, and history of the discrepancy.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Actual work must follow applicable law, approved site procedures, trained personnel, and authorized decisions. Classroom safety discussions do not authorize isolation, servicing, or restart.
